It described increased military influence despite demilitarization rhetoric, centralization of power and weakening of independent checks, an insufficient nearshoring strategy, deteriorating security, personal austerity and persistent anti-corruption rhetoric. It also credited restrained pandemic spending with avoiding a significant fiscal crisis and credited him with successfully negotiating the USMCA.", "question_ids": ["2B.10", "3B.10", "4B.10", "4B.4", "5B.1", "5B.10", "5B.3", "8B.10", "8B.7"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Published July 2023 and directly evaluates the administration before the 2024 transition.", "ruler_attribution": "The assessment concerns López Obrador’s presidency and policies.", "limitations": ["The report is an analytical assessment rather than an official audit; some claims rely on cited secondary sources."], "start_unit": 5, "end_unit": 7, "locator": "page 5 through page 7", "exact_excerpt": "Introduction\nPresident Andrés Manuel López Obrador, known widely by the initials AMLO, aimed to transform\nthe politics, economy, and security of Mexico. He calls his wide-ranging series of reforms the\n“fourth transformation,” placing it on par with other great moments in Mexican history: Mexico’s\nindependence, the Mexican Revolution, and the founding of the modern Mexican state. AMLO is nothing if\nnot ambitious, envisaging himself in the same echelon as Mexico’s national heroes Benito Juárez, Porfirio Díaz,\nand—AMLO’s personal favorite—Lazaro Cárdenas. But as his sexenio—Mexico’s six-year presidential term—ends\nand AMLO has no possibility for reelection, it is time to take stock of his legacy. A full accounting of what\nMexico has experienced and what kind of Mexico AMLO will leave his successor is in dire order. What will\nMexico look like in 2024?\nAt times, AMLO appears to be bulletproof, though his policies, even through a favorable lens, have had\nmixed results at best. At worst, they have proved counterproductive and even hypocritical. He speaks\nof environmental stewardship while pouring money into the oil industry and overseeing an uptick in\ndeforestation. He proclaims a demilitarized approach to security while expanding the military’s influence over\ndaily life and wielding a new national guard gendarmerie indiscriminately. He harkens to antiestablishment\nand populist credentials while centralizing power and defanging independent institutions meant to check the\nexecutive. Indeed, by a staggering array of metrics, from citizen security to rural development to democratic\naccountability, AMLO’s policies actively work against his constituents and self-professed policy objectives.\nNevertheless, AMLO’s personal austerity and a persistent rhetoric of anti-corruption mean his approval ratings\nremain among the highest in Latin America. Even after an unprecedented 8 percent drop in approval following\nhis attack on the popular National Electoral Institute (INE), his approval continued to hover at 60 percent.1\nAMLO’s policies have, in turn, influenced Mexico’s economic, security, and political context with uneven and, at\ntimes, paradoxical results. Mexico is inattentive to and seemingly uninterested in nearshoring, lacking a defined\n1 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nstrategy for capitalizing on shifting global supply chains and embroiled in a set of unproductive trade disputes\nwith its North American neighbors. At the same time, the nation is home to a dynamic and vibrant economy,\nwhich has seen growth in key sectors, from traditional manufacturing to emerging digital technologies and\nsemiconductors. In terms of security, the forecast is bleak, with efforts to curb organized crime deteriorating\nto the point of spurring harmful proposals from the U.S. Congress to authorize the use of military force\nagainst Mexico’s cartels. Nevertheless, a glimmer of potential remains, as long-standing linkages between U.S.\nand Mexican security forces continue to encourage cooperation in the form of the U.S.-Mexico Bicentennial\nFramework for Security, Public Health and Safe Communities.2 Politically, AMLO’s influence has proved corrosive\nto Mexican democracy, presiding over the country’s decline in a few short years from “flawed democracy” to\n“hybrid regime,” according to the Economist Intelligence Unit.3 However, the opposition AMLO has encountered\nto some of his most deleterious political projects suggests a civil society that remains fiercely dedicated to\nprotecting its hard-won democracy, along with nontrivial checks from both the legislative and judicial branches.\nWith fewer than two years remaining in his six-year term, a retrospective is warranted to assess the results\nof the AMLO years, what is yet to come, and what trends will persist long after López Obrador has exited the\nPalacio Nacional. The objective of such an analysis is not to make predictions about who will succeed AMLO\nbut rather to adumbrate the contours of a post-AMLO Mexico in three important dimensions: economy,\nsecurity, and politics.\nIn short, Mexico in 2024 looks economically stagnant despite the once-in-a-generation opportunity presented\nby nearshoring. The nation is weak and debilitated in its fight against ruthless and expanding cartels and\nlimping more than sprinting as democratic institutions continue to suffer debilitating attacks. Overall, the\nMexico of 2024 appears far more battered and bruised than the Mexico AMLO took the reins of in 2018.\n2 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer\n\nEconomy\nPresident López Obrador’s economic policies have been marked by significant variation. On the\none hand, his domestic policy has revolved around a series of social programs that suggest the\ngovernment’s improved commitment to providing a social safety net. On the other, during the\npandemic, Mexico was austere in its spending, devoting a fraction of what other large economies in the region\ncommitted to economic stimulus and judiciously avoiding fiscal crises from increased public debt.4 AMLO\nhas derided “neoliberalism” in all its forms, using this critique as a capacious tent under which to move to\ngreater nationalization of industries and revitalization of often sclerotic state-run enterprises. AMLO has also\nweaponized austerity to dismantle institutions and agencies he does not like.5\nTo his credit, he successfully negotiated the U.S.-Mexico-Canada Agreement (USMCA) with the administration\nunder U.S. president Donald Trump, helping to create one of the world’s most modern trade frameworks.\nFurther, Mexico remains a powerhouse as the second-largest economy in Latin America, with promise in both\ntraditional and emerging sectors. However, according to the World Bank’s Global Economic Prospects report,\nMexico’s economy is projected to grow just 0.9 percent in 2023, the lowest rate of growth in all Latin America.6\nAs Mexico continues on the long path to post-pandemic recovery, it has become increasingly apparent that\nmuch of the country’s economic growth is occurring, as one of the authors notes, “in spite of Mexico’s\ngovernment’s policies, rather than because of them.”7\nUltimately, restoration of confidence from investors and the business community will influence Mexico’s\neconomic position after AMLO according to four main themes: the status of energy reform, Mexico’s ability\nto take advantage of the nearshoring moment, the success of AMLO in delivering on promises to develop the\ncountry’s southern and rural areas, and Mexico’s future bureaucratic and regulatory composition.\n3 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024", "excerpt_sha256": "20e89c343754c2fab849aa026a82735543e0b3f2d8156a60325b45c9db1f44f6", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt does not explicitly support the broader claim that security deterioration was caused by the administration, beyond its analytical assessment of policy effects."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0051-E014", "source_id": "SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1", "url": "https://csis-website-prod.s3.amazonaws.com/s3fs-public/2023-07/230718_Berg_After_AMLO_0.pdf", "title": "After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024", "publisher": "Center for Strategic and International Studies", "raw_sha256": "112c3fb2368e464f70403bf4f07af82fb3b49ec04385a56a31a037931a4b98bd", "fact_summary": "López Obrador halted new foreign oil contracts, audited more than 100 previous awards for corruption and committed billions to the Dos Bocas refinery, but the refinery was reported substantially over budget. His proposed electricity reform would have strengthened state control and subordinated autonomous regulators; Congress failed to approve it, limiting his ability to enact the full agenda. Lithium nationalization and hostility to foreign energy investment raised concerns about investor confidence and clean-energy transition.", "question_ids": ["5B.1", "5B.10", "5B.3", "5B.4", "5B.5", "5B.6", "8B.10", "8B.2", "8B.6", "8B.7", "8B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The assessment covers policies and legislative outcomes through 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The decisions and legislative initiative were attributed to López Obrador’s administration.", "limitations": ["The report includes forecasts and estimates as well as completed policy actions."], "start_unit": 8, "end_unit": 9, "locator": "page 8 through page 9", "exact_excerpt": "Energy Reform\nPresident López Obrador undoubtedly has a keen appreciation for the centrality of energy to Mexican politics\nand civil society. Indeed, he was carried into power in no small part by the outpouring of popular discontent\nagainst the Peña Nieto administration in 2017 as protests erupted against rising gas prices. The spike in\ngasoline prices, known as a gasolinazo, was the sharpest rise in Mexico in two decades.8 Overall, prices in\nsome areas rose by 48 percent as the government under President Enrique Peña Nieto passed a major energy\nreform that ended the legal monopoly of Petróleos Mexicanos (Pemex), Mexico’s state-owned oil company.\nPemex holds the dubious title of the world’s most indebted state-run oil firm and is widely reported to lack\nthe funds or technology to fully develop Mexico’s oil reserves (the country ranks 21st in the world in terms of\ncrude oil resources).9 Nevertheless, the dramatic effect of the gasolinazo protests served to reinforce lessons\nthat López Obrador, a long-standing opponent of privatization, had already taken to heart: the energy reforms\nof his predecessor must be rolled back, and gas prices must remain stable.\nAMLO quickly set about working to realize these goals, placing a hold on new contracts for foreign oil\ncompanies and auditing over 100 of the awards signed under Peña Nieto for corruption.10 At the same time,\nin the early months of his presidency, AMLO declared an $8 billion investment for a new Pemex refinery to be\nlocated in his home state of Tabasco, a commitment later supplemented with a $600 million investment from\nChinese state banks.11 Expected to become operational in 2023, the refinery project has already run some $3\nbillion over budget.12\nView of the Mexican oil company PEMEX’s oil complex in Tula, Hidalgo state, Mexico, on January 11, 2018.\nPhoto Source: Pedro Pardo/AFP/Getty Images\nBy far the most ambitious project the president has undertaken is a proposed constitutional reform that would\nsignificantly strengthen the state’s role in the energy sector. Under the proposed law, Mexico’s state-owned\npower company, the Comisión Federal de Electricidad (CFE), would have a guaranteed 54 percent market\nshare while limiting the participation of private electricity suppliers. It would also fold the autonomous Energy\nRegulatory Commission (CRE) and the National Hydrocarbons Commission (CNH) into the federal government\nby making them subordinate to the Ministry of Energy.13\n4 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer\n\nThe amendment proved a rallying cry for domestic opposition, which cited inefficiencies within the CFE that,\nby some estimates, would increase operating costs by as much as $3 billion a year while increasing the risk\nof power outages by as much as 30 percent.14 The amendment also sparked major concerns internationally,\nespecially among U.S. energy firms, which voiced fears about the stability of their previous investments.\nUltimately, AMLO was unable to muster the political capital to move the initiative forward, as the lower house\nof Congress failed to reach the two-thirds supermajority needed to advance a constitutional amendment.15 The\nfailure of the reform showcased hard limits on AMLO’s power to drive sweeping legislative change in critical\neconomic sectors, providing a greater degree of clarity for prospective investors and businesses.\nHowever, the defeat of the energy reform amendment was far from an unmitigated victory for opponents. In\nApril 2022, Mexico’s Supreme Court issued an ambiguous decision on an earlier energy law that gave the CFE\npriority access to send its energy to the grid before foreign energy producers.16 Ultimately, AMLO’s handling of\nenergy policy impelled the United States and Canada to seek joint consultations under the USMCA in July 2022.\nThus far, the question of energy policy has proved a thorny issue, with the specter of a dispute resolution\npanel raising lingering questions for Mexico’s domestic industry and external investors alike. It also threatens\nto stall nearshoring momentum.\nHigh energy prices coupled with the CFE’s heavy dependency on oil for power generation mean companies\nstruggle to obtain cost-effective power and meet their environmental, social, and corporate governance (ESG)\ntargets while investing in Mexico. Furthermore, in the wake of the amendment’s defeat, AMLO appears to have\nshifted his attention toward the nationalization of other industries. On April 17, the same day as the vote in the\nlower house, the president introduced a reform to the mining law to nationalize Mexico’s lithium industry. The\nlaw was fast-tracked through Congress and approved just two days later.17\nTHE ENERGY OUTLOOK FOR 2024\nThe nationalization of lithium will likely set Mexico back in the transition to lower carbon emissions and the\npush for climate-smart energy solutions. When coupled with AMLO’s hostility to foreign energy companies,\nmany of which seek to invest in renewable power sources, Mexico has locked itself into a traditional carbon-\nand-oil energy strategy for the near future. Indeed, Pemex is a mainstay of AMLO’s fourth transformation\ndomestic project. Mexico’s ability to diversify this strategy in 2024 will depend, in part, on the extent to which\ninvestor confidence can be restored by resolving Mexico’s energy uncertainty through USMCA arbitration and\nthe willingness of AMLO to strenuously oppose future foreign investments in the energy sector.18 Not only does\nthis policy impair Mexico’s ability to meet clean energy and environmental goals, it also reverses one of the most\npositive trends to come out of the 2013 reform, which prompted a windfall of investment in renewable energy\nindustries.19 Lithium may buck this trend but only if the national lithium company proves capable of rapidly and\nefficiently scaling up production, an unlikely prospect as only one lithium mining project, the Chinese-owned\nBacanora Lithium, appears to stand a realistic chance of beginning production by the end of 2023.20\nAnother key concern in the near term is gasoline price stability at a time of rising global oil prices. While\nAMLO will likely do everything in his power to avoid a gasolinazo under his administration, his energy policies\nwill become increasingly untenable over the medium and long term as the administration pours more funds\ninto subsidized fuel. A deterioration of dispute resolution procedures under the USMCA will only exacerbate\nsuch a downward trend, potentially exposing Mexico to punitive tariffs and stifling necessary investment\nin sectors needed for the energy transition. Thus, AMLO may successfully stave off an energy crisis under\nhis administration, but absent a significant course correction when a future crisis hits, it may produce even\ngreater disruptions than those observed in 2017.\n5 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024", "excerpt_sha256": "1102c7f963b73f9548d67bfe409b9ce0ad3b90c4c2f60d0df073ad6167accac3", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0051-E016", "source_id": "SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1", "url": "https://csis-website-prod.s3.amazonaws.com/s3fs-public/2023-07/230718_Berg_After_AMLO_0.pdf", "title": "After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024", "publisher": "Center for Strategic and International Studies", "raw_sha256": "112c3fb2368e464f70403bf4f07af82fb3b49ec04385a56a31a037931a4b98bd", "fact_summary": "Tax collection and fiscal management improved in some respects: the tax-to-GDP ratio rose 1.6 percentage points between 2019 and 2020, Mexico maintained manageable debt and a balanced budget in 2022, and restrained pandemic spending avoided a large primary deficit. Of all the\neconomic shifts listed, these changes to the character and operations of Mexico’s federal system are perhaps\nmost likely to endure long after López Obrador leaves office.\nMany of these developments are promising, especially in the realm of tax collection. Mexico’s tax-to-GDP ratio\nincreased by 1.6 percent between 2019 and 2020, no small feat given the massive disruptions caused by the\nCovid-19 outbreak.49 Thus far, growth in tax revenues has been driven by a fairly modest tax policy, characterized\nby an increased focus on collection rather than real increases in taxes. These gains have allowed Mexico to\ndeliver on a balanced budget with manageable levels of public debt in 2022.50 Should Mexico adopt higher taxes\nin the coming years, this focus on increased state capacity will prove a boon for government revenues.\nAMLO’s spending policy during the height of the pandemic is also worthy of close consideration, as he broke\nranks with many of his peers in the region. While several countries spent heavily on social programs during\nhealth-related lockdowns, Mexico was restrained. Indeed, the dramatic rise in social spending and the\ncorresponding need to make up for sharp budgetary shortfalls has resulted in severe fiscal crises from Chile to\nColombia, typically accompanied by major popular unrest. By contrast, Mexico’s conservative stance allowed\nthe government to avoid a significant primary deficit in the short term. However, this policy also came at a\ncost, as the country had a net loss of around 400,000 businesses in 2020.51 Coupled with an 8.5 percent drop\nin GDP during the pandemic, Mexico’s recovery has proved slower than anticipated.\nIn 2022 the Mexican economy grew by 3 percent, outperforming pessimistic forecasts but ultimately falling\nshort of what is needed to recoup losses, while 2023 promises even smaller growth rates. The consequences\nof AMLO’s fiscal austerity have also fallen unevenly across the government, as López Obrador has systemically\ncut budgets and centralized agencies he views as hostile toward his political project. Perhaps the most notable\ninstance involves the proposed cuts to the INE, which would have hamstrung the agency’s ability to organize\nfair and competitive elections. However, AMLO’s budgetary warfare has ranged broadly, including proposals to\nconsolidate the functions of key transparency watchdogs like the National Institute for Transparency, Access to\nInformation and Personal Data Protection (INAI) into the Secretariat of the Civil Service.52\nA final noteworthy development in Mexico’s institutional landscape under López Obrador has been his efforts to\nsystematically position the military in development and economic roles. The most notable instance of this came\nbefore AMLO assumed the presidency, when he helped organize a referendum against the proposed construction\nof the Mexico City Texcoco Airport. Subsequent responsibility for the construction and maintenance of a new\ninternational airport was handed over to the Secretariat of National Defense (SEDENA). Since then, the new\nairport has become AMLO’s darling, despite repeated setbacks and concerns regarding adherence to international\nstandards for operation.53 The move not only saw the reshuffling of a major infrastructure contract into military\nhands but also will likely give SEDENA veto power over future development and infrastructure spending in and\naround the airport and Mexico City more broadly. Meanwhile, a state-owned company controlled by the Secretariat\nof the Navy (SEMAR) has been entrusted with rehabilitating hundreds of miles of rail lines, including the CIIT.54\nDelegation of infrastructure projects to the armed forces has met with resistance, both within SEDENA and\nSEMAR, as well as civil society and the opposition in Congress. Nevertheless, the awarding of these contracts is\nlikely to create strong factions within the military in support of their expanded role, which may subsequently\ncreate a fearsome roadblock to future efforts at privatization. Lastly, recent legislation passed in a midnight\n10 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer\n\nsession of the Senate gives the army a portion of the country’s tourism tax to a company established by the\narmy to run critical infrastructure such as the new airport and the Maya Train.55\nSTATE CAPACITY AND BUREAUCRATIC OUTLOOK FOR 2024\nMexico appears headed toward a potential fiscal crunch.56 While pet projects come and go, undone just as easily\nas they were conceived, the fiscal challenges they create are much harder to solve. Increased spending, especially\non social (noncontributory) pensions, alongside reduced taxation on state-owned enterprises, especially Pemex,\nhas resulted in a budgetary mismatch thus far allayed by impressive improvements in tax collection.57 In all\nlikelihood, López Obrador will seek to delay emergence of a significant primary deficit until after 2024. However,\nit seems equally unlikely he will take drastic steps to reverse course on fiscal policy by, for instance, significantly\nraising taxes or heavily rolling back social spending. AMLO’s signature projects have proved costly, and most have\ninvolved cost overruns. Thus, whatever successor government arrives in 2024 will have an even shorter timeline\nwith which to make ends meet. An unexpectedly severe recession in the United States could further accelerate\nthis timeline by dramatically reducing U.S. demand for Mexico’s manufacturing exports. Policymakers, however,\nwill be assisted by a more competent tax authority, which may yet soften the blow.\nFigure 3: Budget Transferred to the Armed Forces, 2018–2021\nAll figures are in MXN\nPublic Security\n$65,809,803,880.00\nFederation (MXN)\n$71,815,576,849.89\n(MXN)\nPublic Works\n$4,897,906,655.05 (MXN)\nEnvironmental Protection\nFederal Entities $553,792,640.58 (MXN)\n$1,101,788,113.88 Social Policy\n(MXN)\n$1,638,661,788.14 (MXN)\nSource: \"Plataforma de Proyección de Datos Abiertos (PPData),\" [Open data projection platform], Política de Drogas, https://ppdata.\npoliticadedrogas.org/#ppd.inm.\n11 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nThe role of the military in spearheading infrastructure projects will present a similar question to a post-AMLO\ngovernment. More recently, AMLO’s\ndecision to order SEMAR to conduct a “temporary occupation” of a privately operated rail line along the\nIsthmus of Tehuantepec should be cause for even greater concern, suggesting a role for the military not merely\nin operating public infrastructure projects but also in the seizure and administration of heretofore private\nfacilities.58 If such moves become more commonplace, the militarization of Mexico’s economy will only further\nweaken investor confidence and depress economic growth.\nSignificant questions remain as to how entrenched these interests will be by 2024 and what clashes may occur\nwithin the army and navy on matters of professional ethics and the role of the armed forces. For the first\ntime, PRI, PAN, and the Party of the Democratic Revolution (PRD) combined obtained only 40 percent of the\nvote, compared to their previous 90 percent share.133 Morena has benefited from incredible popular support\nsince, though signs of decline began to emerge in the 2021 midterm elections.\nThe midterm elections decreased Morena’s supermajority to an absolute majority, which was interpreted by\nmany political analysts as positive for Mexico.134 In fact, the elections were marked by high attendance at polls\nand absence of fraud and violence. More than half of the population voted against Morena, suggesting the desire\nfor more options and willingness to support democracy.135 Although Morena won most gubernatorial races, the\nopposition made significant gains in the Chamber of Deputies.136 This came as a wake-up call for the opposition,\nrevealing its potential to represent an alternative to Morena if unified in a coalition. Even if Morena won more\nrepresentatives, it lost in bigger cities, which might indicate a decrease in support within the middle-class electorate\nit used to appeal to. Following AMLO’s repression of feminist protests, the women’s electorate also turned on him,\nanother sign of emerging disillusion with Morena that could have repercussions in future elections.\nOn April 10, 2022, AMLO held the first recall referendum in the history of Mexico. Both Morena and the president\ncampaigned in favor of the referendum and AMLO remaining in charge but reduced the budget of the INE for\ncarrying out such a referendum from 18 million pesos to 4 million. The lack of a clear legal framework; the\nopacity of the referendum’s text, whether in favor of or against AMLO; the budget scandal; and the illegal active\nparticipation of the political party involved in the organization of the referendum affected its legitimacy and\ncitizens’ participation. Less than 20 percent of the population voted, which undermined the referendum’s legality,\nas it required a threshold of 40 percent to be constitutionally valid.137 Further, 91.86 percent of those who voted\nexpressed support for AMLO, who considered it a major victory. His reaction to the results also led many to\nspeculate about his hidden intent to use the referendum as an instrument to prolong his mandate after 2024.\nIn 2023 AMLO’s approval rate remained around 60 percent, according to most opinion polls.138 Evidently,\nmany are still attracted to AMLO’s welfare program and direct contact with citizens through his mañaneras\n(daily press conferences), seduced by his narrative of helping the poor against corruption and the elites. Many\nrevere the president’s personal austerity and common touch. Others have instead realized that the state of\nthe Mexican economy and security is far worse now than before AMLO came into office and that his welfare\nprogram conceals cuts to healthcare and education and presents financial irregularities, with evidence of\ncorruption and wasted resources.139\nIn the context of fast-approaching presidential elections, AMLO’s goal is one of maintaining popularity and\nensuring his party remains in power after he finishes his mandate. His challenge lies in institutionalizing the\nparty and forging a stable path for succession, while convincing the polarized electorate that Morena still\nincarnates the alternative to the political establishment. As of writing, Morena had not yet chosen its official\ncandidate for 2024, but two figures had emerged as the most likely to replace AMLO.140\nClaudia Sheinbaum, current mayor of Mexico City, is AMLO’s favorite and the most likely to continue his\nagenda and style. However, her support, even within Morena’s electorate, is hampered by scandals, accidents,\n25 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nand criticism of her management of the public transportation system in the Mexican capital.141 She could\nappeal to AMLO’s base but would likely struggle to reunite those who are disillusioned with Morena and are\nstarting to look for other options.\nMarcelo Ebrard, current minister of foreign affairs, is seen as the preferred candidate among Morena\nsupporters.142 He is perceived as a competent figure both within and outside Morena and is more independent\nfrom AMLO than Sheinbaum. He could easily appeal to the middle class and convince a broader electorate.143 If\nhe is not chosen as Morena’s candidate, he may run as an independent candidate, which would make the 2024\nelectoral campaign even more dynamic.\nAMLO has also indicated a few other candidates are fit for pursuing his political project: Adán Augusto López\nHernández, secretary of the interior, and Senator Ricardo Monreal Ávila.144 Given there is no established\nmechanism for party primaries, AMLO seems to prefer a set of opinion polls that will make it easier to vote\nfor Sheinbaum, even though such a procedure may trigger questions about transparency and lead to one of\nthe candidates running as an independent.145 Neither candidate, however, possesses the same charisma and\npersonal touch as AMLO, meaning Morena’s narrative could still shift away from AMLO’s.\nFigure 9: Potential Candidate Approval Ratings among Party Members\nMorena’s Coalition\nClaudia Sheinbaum\nMarcelo Ebrard\nRicardo Monreal\nGerardo Fernández Noroña\nAdán Augusto López\nVa Por México\nMiguel Ángel Mancera\nLilly Téllez\nSantiago Creel\nBeatriz Paredes\nClaudia Ruiz Massieu\nSilvano Aureoles\nMovimiento Ciudadano\nLuis Donaldo Colosio Riojas\nEnrique Alfaro\nSamuel García\n0% 5% 10% 15% 20% 25% 30% 35%\nSource: Jorge Buendía, “Encuesta: Morena sigue adelante y gana con cualquier corcholata” [Poll: Morena remains ahead and wins\nwith any aspirant], El Universal, May 5, 2022, https://www.eluniversal.com.mx/nacion/encuesta-morena-sigue-adelante-y-gana-con-\ncualquier-corcholata/.\n26 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer\n\nMorena has also benefited from the opposition not yet forming a cohesive alternative for Mexican citizens.\nThe opposition currently holds just two out of the ten most populous states and is experiencing difficulties in\ndefining its leader.146 Yet, PAN-PRI-PRD have confirmed that they will run together as the alliance called Va Por\nMéxico, and PAN will be in charge of finding the best candidates to challenge Morena.147 AMLO’s recent blatant\nattacks on Mexico’s democratic institutions have helped the opposition reorient and reframe its strategy. Polls\nfrom January 2023 reveal that the opposition currently has an approval rating of 39 percent.148 Further threats\nto democracy could make the opposition stronger and increase its popular support. If Movimiento Ciudadano\n(MC) were to join the opposition coalition, the chances of beating Morena would be much higher. MC could\ntip the balance given its policies are similar to those of PAN and PRI, it appeals to young urban voters, and it\ncurrently controls two of Mexico’s most important states.149\nTHE ELECTORAL OUTLOOK FOR 2024\nWhile the question of who will be president in 2024 cannot be answered with any degree of certainty, the June\n2023 gubernatorial elections for the State of Mexico and Coahuila nevertheless carry important implications. In\nthe State of Mexico, a shaky coalition among the opposition parties contributed to a win by Morena candidate\nDelfina Gómez, who garnered a 52.7 percent majority despite having been found guilty of stealing pay from\nfederal employees to support her party during a previous bid for governor. While Morena’s victory in a state\nlong viewed as a PRI bastion has been cast as a sign of the inevitability of a Morena president in 2024, the party’s\ndefeat by the opposition Va por México coalition in Coahuila suggests such predictions are far from set in stone\nand underscores the value of coalition-building and effective candidate selection as key ingredients going into\n2024.150 In Coahuila, Morena was beset by difficulties and demonstrated lack of discipline, unceremoniously\ndumping candidates and making last-minute adjustments to lists after lagging in the polls. The lack of unity\namong the opposition and current opinion polls still indicate the likelihood of a Morena 2.0 mandate, at least as\nfar as the presidency is concerned. Nevertheless, challenges persist for AMLO in picking a successor, with the\npossibility of an internal split within Morena. When it comes to Congress, Morena seems likely to lose ground,\nushering in a possibly contentious environment dominated by obstructionism, public dissent, and inability to\nadvance policy. Should a successor from Morena ascend to the presidency, they will also need to grapple with\nAMLO's legacy, which already promises to exercise significant influence as all the party's current aspirants voice\ntheir support for creating a new \"Ministry of the Fourth Transformation\" (potentially headed by AMLO's son) to\ncarry on the outgoing president's vision for the country.\nU.S.-Mexico Relations\nAMLO’s friendly attitude toward Trump from 2018 to 2020 obscured an anti-American agenda. This became\neven clearer once President Joseph Biden was elected and AMLO refused to congratulate him.151 The two\ncountries are now maintaining a “strained partnership” characterized by unsuccessful diplomatic efforts and\ncontroversies over energy policy.152 In fact, contrary to the equal treatment for private companies and path\ntoward renewable energy established in the USMCA, AMLO proceeded to nationalize energy companies and\nlithium production. Canadian and U.S. concerns ended up in formal dispute settlement mechanisms, which\nbegan in July 2022.\nAMLO faces a dilemma regarding the USMCA. Increased economic cooperation with the United States and\nCanada has been interpreted as a sign of stability, boosting international investments in Mexico.153 Yet AMLO’s\nanti-American sentiment has shaped his agenda and has been exploited as a political element. To him, personal\npopularity and the question of his successor matter more than the relationship with the United States.154\n27 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nMigration issues also heavily shape the bilateral relationship. During his administration, Trump threatened\nto impose 5 percent tariffs on imports from Mexico if the country did not get more involved in controlling\nmigration from Central America. An agreement was reached providing for Mexico’s contribution to controlling\nmigration in exchange for noninterference from the United States in internal Mexican issues and AMLO’s way\nof governing.155 With authorities counting 2.76 million undocumented migrants in 2022, Biden has underscored\nhow Trump’s compromise with AMLO does not work anymore, though he has been reluctant to address\nMexican internal affairs.156\nU.S. president Joe Biden (L) is welcomed by his Mexican counterpart AMLO upon landing at Felipe Ángeles\nInternational Airport in Zumpango de Ocampo, north of Mexico City on January 8, 2023.\nPhoto Source: Claudio Cruz/AFP/Getty Images\nAnother controversial area in the U.S.-Mexico relationship touches on drug enforcement cooperation and\nsecurity. Both countries appear to be talking at cross-purposes. The United States is deeply concerned with\ndrug trafficking, especially fentanyl coming from Mexico, which is responsible for the vast majority of the over\n107,000 overdose deaths reported in the United States for 2021.157 Mexico, for its part, is primarily concerned\nwith the flow of guns from the United States and recently sued the United States for alleged failure to curb\nillegal arms trafficking to Mexico.158 Cooperation in this field has been undermined by several actions taken by\nAMLO’s government. In 2020 the National Security Law responded to U.S. prosecution of Mexican officials for\ncollaboration with illegal drug traffickers by limiting the scope of action of U.S. drug enforcement agents on\nMexican territory. In 2021 the Mexican government shut down an investigative unit that worked closely with\nthe DEA, complicating the agency’s task of passing sensitive information to Mexican agents.159\nWith the new Bicentennial Framework launched in January 2022 replacing the Mérida Initiative, which defined\nU.S.-Mexico security cooperation for over a decade, Mexico and the United States have tried to address organized\ncrime less from a military perspective and more from a public health and developmental perspective, together with\nactions to target labs and extradite leaders.160 However, InSight Crime criticizes the plan for remaining very similar\nto the Mérida Initiative.161 The plan’s kingpin strategy, with its excessive focus on targeting leaders, might increase\ninsecurity, as followers are as important as leaders in avoiding fractures within and fights between drug cartels.162\n28 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer\n\nSeveral other episodes have yielded an uneven track record for the diplomatic relationship between Mexico\nand the United States. In 2022, for instance, AMLO boycotted the Summit of the Americas hosted in Los\nAngeles. Officially, he cited opposition to the barring of Nicaragua, Cuba, and Venezuela from the summit,\nthough his refusal was likely linked to a desire to avoid critics of Mexico’s internal affairs.163 The White House\nmeeting between AMLO and Biden in July 2022 reassured the existence and importance of Mexico-U.S. ties.\nDespite their different styles, Mexico and the United States agreed on the need to work more closely together,\nreduce barriers to trade, and tackle food insecurity and immigration. Immediately after the meeting, Mexico\nagreed to spend over $1.5 billion on border infrastructure between 2022 and 2024.164\nFollowing the U.S.-Mexico High-Level Economic Dialogue in 2022, the two countries committed to enhance\ntheir supply chains and reduce risk of disruptions by nearshoring the manufacturing of critical products, such\nas semiconductors and personal protective equipment (PPE), to Mexico and addressing critical minerals.165\nThe North American Leaders’ Summit in January 2023 was also deemed positive by observers for advancing\ncooperation, though it still took place under a cloud of tense trade negotiations.166\nDespite this gradual diplomatic rapprochement, the United States and Mexico appear ideologically divergent.\nAMLO has expressed a wish to see the Organization of American States reformed, accusing it of being\nexcessively dominated by the United States. The Mexico State Morena Youth group has supported the\nRussian invasion of Ukraine, while AMLO has criticized NATO’s actions. At the same time, Mexico has signed\nan agreement with Russia on space cooperation and has diplomatically supported dictatorships such as in\nVenezuela. This has further complicated every other aspect of the U.S.-Mexico partnership.167\nTHE BILATERAL OUTLOOK FOR 2024\nAlthough Biden has not been vocal that the U.S.-Mexico partnership is in danger and has opted not to criticize\nAMLO’s domestic practices, AMLO has brought Mexico ideologically further from the United States than it\nhas been in a long time. On the one hand, security cooperation and diplomacy have faced several ups and\ndowns that can be expected to continue in the final year of the AMLO and Biden administrations, given\ntheir inherently different styles and political agendas. On the other hand, trade and economic integration\nhave reached very high levels, with the two sides talking about aligning incentives in key industries such as\nsemiconductors. In 2022 Mexico was the United States’ second-largest trading partner in the trade of goods\nand services, while the United States accounted for 80 percent of Mexican goods exports.168 Recent increases\nin bilateral trade portend a trend that is likely to remain. In addition, with growing opportunities to relocate\nindustries from Asia to Latin America to avoid supply chain disruptions, Mexico has become key in attracting\ninvestors, underlining the expected expansion of U.S.-Mexico economic relations in the near future.169\nIn 2024 the core issues for the U.S.-Mexico relationship are anticipated to remain the same: Mexico and the\nUnited States will have to cooperate on trade, investment, full implementation of the USMCA, drug trafficking,\nsecurity, migration, and especially border control. However, the way in which such cooperation will manifest\ncould vary significantly. In 2024 both the United States and Mexico will elect new presidents. AMLO’s departure\ncould already be significant for the partnership, and no matter his success in designating a successor, whoever\nenters the Palacio Nacional next year will undoubtedly seek to leave their mark on Mexico’s foreign policy.\nMexican domestic and foreign policy—in particular the extent to which Mexico manages to control its borders—\ncould, in turn, affect U.S. elections, as border policy remains one of the Republican Party’s main concerns.\nMeanwhile, proponents within the U.S. Congress who have pushed legislative initiatives authorizing military\nforce, including air strikes, against criminal organizations operating in Mexico would do well to remember one\nanalyst’s observation: “Mexico is a target-rich environment, not for U.S. missiles, but for sanctions.”170\n29 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nIn addition, political relations will determine the extent to which Mexico can secure nearshoring\nopportunities, as well as investments in minerals, semiconductors, and other key industries that now seem to\nbroaden the potential of U.S.-Mexico economic relations. At the same time, Mexico may continue to pursue\nits anti-American sentiment aiming to avoid undue interference in internal affairs and seeking the support\nof countries with similar agendas.171 The more the country becomes militarized and the less secure and\ndemocratic it becomes, the more its relations with the United States could be eroded.172 In any case, Mexico\nwill still need pragmatism, as it cannot neglect its dependence on access to the U.S. market and the great\neconomic and trade opportunities established under the USMCA. Ultimately, geographic proximity, economic\ninterdependence, and positive day-to-day cooperation will play a role in keeping the two countries close,\noutlining a bright spot in an otherwise gloomy picture.\n30 | Ryan C. En 2020, solo el 26 % tuvo un diseño adecuado o destacado, mientras que en\n2022 la proporción subió al 50%. De igual manera, aumentó la categoría de moderados, que pasó\ndel 25 % en 2020 al 38 % en 2022, un incremento de 13 puntos porcentuales. También se observa una\ndisminución significativa en los programas con una MIR con oportunidad de mejora, en 2020 ocho\nprogramas formaban parte de este grupo, para 2022 solamente dos se mantienen en este nivel, estos\nprogramas son Universidades para el Bienestar Benito Juárez García (U083) de la SEP y Atención a la\nSalud y Medicamentos Gratuitos para la Población sin Seguridad Social Laboral (U013).\n\n\n\n\n3\n    Consúltese la metodología del diagnóstico de matrices en: https://www.coneval.org.mx/coordinacion/Documents/monitoreo/informes/Diagnostico_MIR_2020.pdf\n\n\n\n\n                                                                Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social        19\n\nDe los programas que en 2020 tenían oportunidad de mejora, Fertilizantes (S292), coordinado por la\nSader, pasó de tener una MIR dentro de esta categoría a una MIR con diseño adecuado. Este cambio\nse explica debido a que el programa logró formular indicadores con un nivel destacado en 2022, es\ndecir, sus indicadores cumplen con los criterios de calidad definidos por el CONEVAL para dar un se-\nguimiento adecuado a sus objetivos. No obstante, aún tiene pendiente analizar si con la sola entrega\nde fertilizantes los productores que forman parte de su población podrían incrementar su producción.\n\n\nPor su parte, Producción para el Bienestar (S293), también coordinado por esa dependencia, pasó\nde tener una MIR con oportunidad de mejora en 2020, a una MIR con diseño destacado en 2022. Lo\nanterior debido a que logró un diseño destacado, tanto en objetivos como en el planteamiento de in-\ndicadores, por tanto, consiguió consolidar su matriz de indicadores con la calidad y solidez suficiente\npara monitorear su desempeño.\n\n\nGráfica 3. Por su parte,\nlos programas Fertilizantes (S292), Producción para el Bienestar (S293), ambos de la Sader, y Jóvenes\nEscribiendo el Futuro (S283) de la SEP, que en 2020 tuvieron indicadores con calidad moderada, en\n2022 lograron una calidad destacada.\n\n\nNo obstante, aún hay programas que necesitan mejorar esta área. En este escenario se encuentra el\nprograma Universidades para el Bienestar Benito Juárez García (U083), que pasó de tener indicadores\ncon calidad moderada en 2020 a indicadores con oportunidad de mejora en 2022. Asimismo, el Pro-\ngrama de Mejoramiento Urbano (S273), se mantiene en la categoría moderada tanto en 2020 y 2022.\n\n\n\n                              20       Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2022: estado actual al cuarto año de la Administración y resultados alcanzados en 2021\n\nPor su parte, Atención a la Salud y Medicamentos Gratuitos para la Población sin Seguridad Social\nLaboral (U013) de la Secretaría de Salud es el único programa prioritario que tiene indicadores con\noportunidad de mejora desde 2020 y no ha logrado superar esta condición.\n\n\nEn la figura 3 se muestra cuál ha sido la transición de la calidad de los indicadores de los programas\nentre el 2020 y 2022. Se observa que el número de programas que cuentan con calidad destacada\naumentó de cuatro en 2020 a ocho en 2022. Dentro de las mejoras también se identifica que en 2020\nninguno de los programas contaba con calidad adecuada en sus indicadores, mientras que en 2022\nexisten tres programas en esta categoría, dos de ellos pasaron de tener semáforo amarillo en 2020 a\nverde en 2022. De igual manera, dos programas que contaban con semáforo rojo en 2020 pasaron\na amarillo en 2022. Por último, en 2022 solo dos programas tienen indicadores con oportunidad de\nmejora, mientras que en 2020 había cinco en esta condición.\n\n\nFigura 3. Para identificar las temáticas de atención de una intervención pública, el CONEVAL realizó un\nanálisis de objetivos centrales (Propósito), a través del cual se determinó si en el Propósito de cada\nprograma se formuló de manera parcial o total una problemática pública a resolver. En este análisis\nse identificaron las siguientes temáticas: bienestar económico, bienestar social, carencias sociales,\nciencia y tecnología, desarrollo cultural, medio ambiente, mejora educativa, mejora en salud, mejora\nlaboral, productividad y gestión. Cabe señalar que si el objetivo carece de claridad en la identifica-\nción del problema público que se pretende resolver se clasifica dentro de la categoría gestión.\n\n\nDe los 16 programas prioritarios vigentes, 12 (75 %) de ellos lograron identificar de manera total o par-\ncial la temática de su problema central y cuatro (25 %) fueron clasificados con un objetivo de gestión,\nes decir, no se logró identificar el resultado esperado en la población.\n\n\nEn la figura 4, se muestra la distribución de programas por temática de atención, donde se observa\nque las más atendidas por los programas prioritarios, sin considerar aquellos cuyo objetivo aún se\nclasifica como de gestión, son el bienestar social y la mejora educativa con tres programas cada una.\n\n\nEn la temática de gestión —donde se incluyen a todos los programas que no tienen claridad en la\nidentificación del problema público que pretenden resolver— se encuentran programas como Univer-\nsidades para el Bienestar Benito Juárez García (U083) Jóvenes Construyendo el Futuro (S280) y Aten-\nción a la Salud y Medicamentos Gratuitos para la Población sin Seguridad Social Laboral (U013). Una\nvez que estos programas definan su objetivo central con enfoque de resultados, se podrían ubicar en\nlas temáticas de mejora educativa, productividad o mejora en salud, respectivamente.\n\n\nUn ejemplo de un programa que logró estructurar su objetivo central con enfoque de resultados fue\nSembrando Vida (S287), que pasó de un objetivo de gestión a uno de atención a carencias sociales.\nEn su Propósito actual, el programa busca que su población objetivo cubra sus necesidades alimen-\nticias básicas a través de la entrega de: apoyos en especie para la producción agroforestal; acom-\npañamiento social y técnico para la implementación del programa y aportaciones económica a sus\nbeneficiarios.\n\n\n\n                                     Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social   23\n\nFigura 4. Tres\nde ellos lo hicieron debido a un cambio de enfoque que experimentaron durante este periodo; por\nejemplo, el Programa Pensión de las Personas con Discapacidad Permanente (S286), pasó de buscar\nmejorar la calidad de vida de su población objetivo, a “buscar mejorar el ingreso monetario” de esta\npoblación.\n\n\nPor su parte, tres programas han ganado claridad en la identificación de su problemática pública\na resolver, al pasar de un objetivo de gestión a una temática claramente identificada. Ejemplo de\nello es Producción para el Bienestar (S293) que pasó de tener dos objetivos centrales4 a definir un\núnico objetivo que busca como resultado que los pequeños y medianos productores incrementen el\nrendimiento de sus cultivos o unidades de producción. Los otros dos programas que mejoraron son\nFertilizantes (S292), que pasó de tener una temática de gestión a una de productividad; y Sembrando\nVida (S287) que pasó de un objetivo de gestión a uno enfocado a resolver la carencia alimentaria\nde su población objetivo.\n\n\n\n\n4\n    En la Metodología de Marco Lógico se establece que el problema que busca resolver una intervención pública debe ser único, ya que el definir más de uno ocasiona que\n    los esfuerzos y recursos del programa se dispersen, además de dificultar la tarea de monitoreo y evaluación. Para más información consultar: https://www.coneval.org.mx/\n    Informes/Coordinacion/Publicaciones%20oficiales/GUIA_PARA_LA_ELABORACION_DE_MATRIZ_DE_INDICADORES.pdf\n\n\n\n\n                                          24              Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2022: estado actual al cuarto año de la Administración y resultados alcanzados en 2021\n\nDos programas sociales perdieron claridad en el resultado que buscan alcanzar: Universidades para\nel Bienestar Benito Juárez García (U083) y Atención a la Salud y Medicamentos Gratuitos para la Po-\nblación sin Seguridad Social Laboral (U013).\n\n\nEl primero perdió claridad en la delimitación del resultado que se busca lograr. Al inicio del programa,\nel propósito fue que “las personas con bachillerato terminado accedieran a los servicios de las univer-\nsidades Benito Juárez”; en 2022 el propósito del programa busca: “Superar la exclusión educativa de\ntipo superior contribuye a disminuir la desigualdad en comunidades y municipios”. Con este objetivo\nse pierde claridad de la población sobre la cual busca generar un resultado; es decir, requiere especi-\nficar las características socioeconómicas y demográficas particulares de las personas que presentan\nel problema que el programa quiere solucionar. En ese caso, hay una oportunidad de mejora en la\nclaridad con que el programa operacionalice el: “superar la exclusión educativa”. Tal como está plan-\nteada, podría registrar sus resultados en diversas formas, por lo que se considera ambigua.\n\n\nEn cuanto al Programa Atención a la Salud y Medicamentos Gratuitos para la Población sin Seguridad\nSocial (U013), al inicio de su operación, su propósito se orientaba a que “la población sin seguridad social\ntuviera acceso efectivo, pleno y equitativo a la atención a la salud y a los medicamentos gratuitos”,\nque formaba parte de la temática de mejora en salud; desde 2021, tiene como objetivo central, la\ntransferencia de recursos a las entidades federativas”. La oportunidad de mejora en el Propósito de\neste programa radica también en la claridad de la población objetivo que presenta el problema, así\ncomo en el cambio o resultado que se busca generar en ella.\n\n\nFigura 5. was outstanding. Several programs deteriorated from 2020, and budget allocation showed almost no relationship to effective target compliance. The report identified weak target-setting, unclear populations and inconsistent sustained performance as continuing challenges.", "question_ids": ["5B.6", "5B.7", "6B.5", "8B.6", "8B.7"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The report evaluates 2021 results and 2022 budgets, serving as evidence about implementation capacity entering 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The programs and budget choices belonged to the federal administration under López Obrador.", "limitations": ["The report does not establish why budget allocations were made or whether later corrective action occurred."], "start_unit": 42, "end_unit": 51, "locator": "page 42 through page 51", "exact_excerpt": "Metodología\n\nCuando se crea un programa o acción social, este responde a un objetivo central o Propósito y a\nciertos objetivos específicos que el gobierno mexicano busca lograr en un determinado periodo.\nPara conocer si el cumplimiento de estos objetivos se está llevando a cabo, los programas formulan\nindicadores y establecen metas, cuya consecución se monitorea a través de estas mediciones. Para\ncontar con una valoración de este proceso, el CONEVAL desarrolló la metodología del Cumplimiento\nEfectivo de Metas (CEM), la cual analiza en qué medida los programas sociales cumplen de manera\nefectiva lo que pretenden lograr. La metodología considera exclusivamente los indicadores de nivel\nPropósito y Componentes que están incluidos en la Matriz de Indicadores para Resultados (MIR) de\nlos programas y acciones sociales.\n\n\nEl CEM, por tanto, valora el cumplimiento de metas de cada indicador, la calidad de estas medicio-\nnes y la congruencia en los ajustes de metas que pudo experimentar el programa por ajustes en su\npresupuesto. El cálculo del CEM se realiza en dos fases: 1) la valoración del nivel de logro de cada\nuno de los indicadores de su MIR asociados al objetivo principal y a los entregables que otorgan a\nsu población beneficiaria y 2) la estimación del CEM expresada en una escala 0-1.13 Lo anterior se\ndescribe en la siguiente figura:\n\n\nFigura 10. Metodología del CEM\n\n\n                                                                         Cumplimiento bruto\n\n\n\n\n                                                             Congruencia en                    Calidad\n                                                            el ajuste de metas\n\n\n\n\n                                                                              Nivel de logro\n                                                                           de cada indicador\n\n\n\n\n                                                                Resultado                       Gestión                  Promedio del nivel del logro\n                          Promedio del nivel del logro\n                         de los indicadores de Propósito                                                 30%         de los indicadores de Componentes\n                                                              70%\n\n\n\n\n                                                                        Cumplimiento efectivo\n                                                                        de metas por programa\n\n\n\n\nFuente: elaborado por el CONEVAL.\n\n13\n     Para consultar la metodología completa del CEM, ver: https://www.coneval.org.mx/coordinacion/Documents/monitoreo/informes/Informe_Cumplimiento_efectivo_de_me-\n     tas_2020.pdf\n\n\n\n\n                                                           Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social      41\n\nCon base en el CEM, los programas y acciones sociales se clasifican en cuatro niveles de cumpli-\nmiento: destacado, adecuado, bajo y deficiente. Cada nivel cuenta con un descriptor que permite\ninterpretar de manera general sus resultados.\n\n\nEs importante resaltar que el CEM captura el cumplimiento de las metas de los indicadores adopta-\ndos por los programas dentro de su Matriz de Indicadores y no considera indicadores externos a este\ninstrumento, por lo que los resultados obtenidos están limitados a lo que el programa refleje en su MIR\ny a las variaciones en su presupuesto.\n\n\nUniverso de análisis\n\nPara estimar el Cumplimiento Efectivo de Metas, como se describió en el apartado metodológico, se\nconsideran solamente los indicadores de nivel Propósito (resultados) y Componente (entrega de bie-\nnes o servicios) de los programas presupuestarios. En 2021,12 los programas prioritarios emplearon 124\nindicadores, tanto de Propósito como de Componente, 27 indicadores más que en 2020.\n\n\nLas proporciones entre ambos niveles de indicadores se mantuvieron relativamente similares en estos\naños, sin embargo, se incrementó la proporción de indicadores de resultados (Propósito) en dos pun-\ntos porcentuales, al pasar de un 44 % (43 indicadores) en 2020 a un 46 % (57 indicadores) en 2021. Por\nconsiguiente, se disminuyó la proporción, no así en términos absolutos, empleada para monitorear la\nentrega de sus bienes o servicios (Componente) al pasar de un 56 % (54 indicadores) en 2020 al 54 %\n(67 indicadores) en 2022.\n\n\nGráfica 10. Por un\nlado, programas como Pensión para el Bienestar de las Personas Adultas Mayores (S176) cuenta con\nuna calificación destacada en el CEM y tiene el mayor presupuesto dentro de los prioritarios, por otro\nlado, programas como Atención a la Salud y Medicamentos Gratuitos para la Población sin Seguridad\nSocial Laboral (U013) tiene el segundo mayor presupuesto, a pesar de tener un CEM deficiente.\n\n\nGráfica 11. 2021 y el año 2022, se\nobserva que la mayoría de los programas prioritarios tuvieron disminuciones en este concepto en\ntérminos reales, a excepción de los programas Fertilizantes (S292), con un incremento del 153%; Pen-\nsión para el Bienestar de las Personas Adultas Mayores (S176) con un aumento del 63% y Pensión para\nel Bienestar de las Personas con Discapacidad Permanente (S286). No obstante, solo el programa\nPensión para el Bienestar de las Personas Adultas Mayores (S176) tuvo un CEM destacado, mientras\nque los otros dos tuvieron un CEM bajo o deficiente. De igual forma, al calcular el coeficiente de\ncorrelación entre el cambio presupuestal y el CEM de los 16 programas prioritarios, este resultó en\n-0.07, lo que podría interpretarse como una relación prácticamente nula entre ambas variables, es\ndecir, que no hay una asociación entre la asignación presupuestaria y el cumplimiento de metas de\nlos programas prioritarios. En este sentido, se propone a los actores responsables de la asignación\npresupuestaria, tomar en cuenta el CEM como parte de los criterios utilizados en el proceso de asig-\nnación presupuestaria de los programas y acciones sociales.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                                                    Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social                                                  43\n\nCumplimiento Efectivo de Metas de los programas\nprioritarios 2020 vs 2021\n\nEl Cumplimiento Efectivo de Metas de los programas prioritarios es muy similar entre 2020 y 2021, pues-\nto que alrededor del 56 % de ellos tuvo un cumplimiento adecuado o destacado en ambos años. No\nobstante, se observan variaciones importantes al revisarlos individualmente.\n\n\nEntre las diferencias, se encontró que, de los cuatro programas que tuvieron un CEM destacado en\n2020, solo Beca Universal para Estudiantes de Educación Media Superior (S311) se mantuvo en esta\ncategoría. De este grupo llaman la atención los programas Sembrando Vida (S287) y el Programa de\nApoyo para el Bienestar de las Niñas y Niños, Hijos de Madres Trabajadoras (S174). El primero pasó\nde estar en un nivel destacado en 2020 a un nivel bajo en 2021; mientras que el Programa de Apoyo\npara el Bienestar de las Niñas y Niños, Hijos de Madres Trabajadoras (S174) pasó de un cumplimiento\ndestacado en 2020 a uno deficiente en 2021; lo anterior debido a que ambos tuvieron una valoración\nbaja en sus indicadores de Propósito, además de reportar un sobrecumplimiento15 en uno de sus in-\ndicadores de este nivel.16\n\n\nPor otro lado, hay cuatro programas que contaban con un nivel bajo o deficiente en su CEM y que\nen 2021 lograron ubicarse en niveles de cumplimiento de metas adecuados o destacados, entre ellos\nse distinguen los dos programas prioritarios coordinados por la Sedatu,17 que en 2020 tenían un CEM\ndeficiente y en 2022 lograron un CEM adecuado. Para revisar el CEM de los programas prioritarios en\n2020 y 2021 consultar el anexo 5.\n\n\nGráfica. 12. resultados alcanzados en 2021\n\nCumplimiento Efectivo de Metas por dependencia\n\nLa gráfica 13 muestra la distribución de los programas y acciones sociales que coordinan las depen-\ndencias y entidades federales, según su CEM. Al examinarla, se identificó que los cuatro programas a\ncargo de la SEP tienen un CEM adecuado o destacado, siendo esta la dependencia con una mayor\nproporción de programas y acciones en este nivel.\n\n\nEn este sentido, destaca la Secretaría de Bienestar, puesto que los programas que coordina tuvieron\nen su mayoría retrocesos en el cumplimiento efectivo de sus metas en relación con 2020. En ese año\nla dependencia contaba con tres programas con nivel destacado y ninguno en deficiente, mientras\nque en 2021 solo contó con un programa destacado y dos deficientes. El único programa de la\ndependencia que logró una mejoría fue Pensión para el Bienestar de las Personas Adultas Mayores\n(S176), que pasó de un cumplimiento bajo a uno destacado; el resto de los programas disminuyo su\nnivel de cumplimiento.\n\n\nPor su parte, la totalidad de los programas de la Sader alcanzó un cumplimiento de metas bajo. El\nprograma Producción para el Bienestar (S293) disminuyó su nivel de cumplimiento, al pasar de ade-\ncuado en 2020 a bajo en 2021; Fertilizantes (S292) aumentó de nivel deficiente a bajo. Por su parte, el\nprograma Precios de Garantía a Productos Alimentarios Básicos (S290) se mantuvo en nivel bajo en\n2020 y 2021.\n\n\nEn contraste, se identifica que los dos programas a cargo de la Sedatu mejoraron su cumplimiento, al\npasar de un nivel bajo en 2020 a uno adecuado en 2021. El único programa prioritario operado por\nSalud; Atención a la Salud y Medicamentos Gratuitos para la Población sin Seguridad Social Laboral\n(U013) es también el único prioritario que mantuvo un CEM deficiente en 2020 y 2021.\n\n\nGráfica 13. Cumplimiento efectivo promedio de metas por dependencia\n\n                 100\n\n                  90                     20\n                                         (1)\n                  80   50\n                       (2)               20\n                  70\n                                         (1)\n    Porcentaje\n\n\n\n\n                  60\n                                         20                         100                         100                     100     100\n                  50\n                                         (1)                        (3)                         (1)                     (2)     (1)\n                  40\n                  30   50\n                  20   (2)               40\n                                         (2)\n                  10\n\n                   0\n                       SEP          Bienestar                     Sader                      Salud                    Sedatu   STPS\n\n                                                Deficiente              Bajo              Adecuado                Destacado\n\nFuente: elaborado por el CONEVAL.\n\n\n\n\n                                                 Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social      45\n\nCumplimiento Efectivo de Metas por temáticas de atención\n\nSi analizamos el Cumplimiento Efectivo de Metas por cada una de las temáticas de atención a las\nque se enfocan los programas sociales, se observa que los tres programas enfocados a atender la\nmejora educativa tuvieron un cumplimiento efectivo de metas destacado o adecuado, es decir, plan-\ntearon metas acordes con su capacidad operacional para lograr sus objetivos.\n\n\nNo obstante, se observa una mala planeación de metas en los programas orientados a promover el\nbienestar económico, como Precios de Garantía a Productos Alimentarios Básicos (S290) y Pensión\npara el Bienestar de las Personas con Discapacidad Permanente (S286). El Programa Precios de Ga-\nrantía a Productos Alimentarios Básicos (S290) tuvo indicadores a nivel de Propósito con valoraciones\nde calidad bajas y niveles de cumplimiento de metas, lo que explica su CEM deficiente. Por su parte,\nPensión para el Bienestar de las Personas con Discapacidad Permanente (S286) tuvo sobrecumpli-\nmientos en dos de sus indicadores de Propósito y un cumplimiento de meta bajo de su indicador de\nComponente: porcentaje de pagos de marcha otorgados a las personas adultas auxiliares de las\npersonas con discapacidad permanente respecto de las bajas de derechohabientes del padrón por\nfallecimiento identificadas, lo que explica su bajo nivel de CEM.\n\n\nAsimismo, los dos programas prioritarios enfocados a promover la productividad cuentan con niveles\nde cumplimiento de metas bajo, estos programas son Fertilizantes (S292) y Producción para el Bien-\nestar (S293), ambos coordinados por la Sader. Finalmente, hay tres programas que no tienen clara su\nproblemática pública a resolver, sin embargo, tuvieron niveles de cumplimiento de metas adecuados.\nTodos ellos tienen un alto potencial para definir con precisión este elemento de su Propósito y en con-\nsecuencia avanzar de manera significativa hacia un diseño sólido de su intervención. Estos progra-\nmas son Universidades para el Bienestar Benito Juárez García (U083), Jóvenes Construyendo el Futuro\n(S280) y Programa Nacional de Reconstrucción (S281).\n\n\n\n\n                       46        Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2022: estado actual al cuarto año de la Administración y resultados alcanzados en 2021\n\nGráfica 14. Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social     47\n\nCapítulo 7.\nConclusiones\n\n\n\n\n               Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2022: estado actual al cuarto año de la Administración y resultados alcanzados en 2021\n\nHallazgos\n\nEn general, los programas prioritarios han logrado identificar cada vez mejor el problema público que\nbuscan resolver y la población objetivo sobre la cual actúan. Por ejemplo, el programa Fertilizantes\n(S292), pasó de definir su objetivo principal como “el aumento en la disponibilidad de fertilizantes\nquímicos y biológicos”, a uno con enfoque de resultados que busca “incrementar la producción de\nlos cultivos prioritarios”.\n\n\nLa mayoría de estos programas tienen indicadores adecuados para medir sus resultados y la entrega\nde sus bienes o servicios, por tanto, son confiables para monitorear su desempeño. Por ejemplo, el\nPrograma para el Bienestar de las Personas en Emergencia Social o Natural (U011) de la Secretaría de\nBienestar pasó de tener indicadores que medían únicamente cobertura, a medir el resultado de las\npersonas en términos de carencia por la falta de acceso diferentes derechos.\n\n\nAsimismo, han tenido avances significativos en el diseño general de sus Matrices de Indicadores para\nResultados (MIR), solo el 13 % (2) de ellos cuenta con un diseño de MIR con oportunidad de mejora. De\nigual forma hay avances importantes en formular sus objetivos centrales con enfoque de resultados.\n\n\nLas problemáticas públicas que atienden los programas prioritarios se pueden dividir en cinco temá-\nticas, siendo la Mejora educativa y el Bienestar social las más atendidas. En cuanto a poblaciones\nobjetivo, los programas prioritarios atienden a seis grupos, dentro de estos los jóvenes son la población\nque es atendida por el mayor número de programas prioritarios. No obstante, la población con mayor\npresupuesto son los adultos mayores, seguida de la población no identificada, en la que se encuen-\ntran programas como Atención a la Salud y Medicamentos Gratuitos para la Población sin Seguridad\nSocial Laboral (U013), que es el segundo prioritario con mayor presupuesto en 2022.\n\n\nLos programas prioritarios tienen un amplio impacto en la población, puesto que sus beneficiarios re-\npresentan el 21 % de la población nacional. Tan solo el programa Becas de Educación Básica para el\nBienestar Benito Juárez (S072) de la SEP sumó a más de 2 millones de beneficiarios entre 2020 y 2021.\n\n\nEn relación con el Cumplimiento Efectivo de Metas de los Programas, se observa que al contemplar\nel promedio total de programas con cumplimiento adecuado o destacado no existen grandes dife-\nrencias entre el año 2020 y el 2021, en ambos años, el CEM es de alrededor del 56 %. Sin embargo, al\nrevisar el cumplimiento de manera particular se encuentra que han existido diversos cambios, por\n\n\n\n                                     Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social   49\n\nejemplo, solo uno de los programas que obtuvo un nivel destacado en 2020 permanece en este nivel\npara 2021; y cuatro programas que tenían nivel bajo o deficiente en 2020 se ubicaron en calificacio-\nnes adecuadas o destacadas para 2021. Esto implica que, a pesar de los avances en programas\ndeficientes, aún existen dificultades para que los programas mantengan un buen cumplimiento a lo\nlargo del tiempo.\n\n\nRetos\n\nDentro de los retos más importantes se encuentra el cumplimiento efectivo de las metas que los pro-\ngramas establecen. A nivel de resultados, se observa un incremento en el número de programas con\nun logro deficiente, lo anterior, debido a que los programas establecieron metas demasiado laxas o\nno reportaron sus resultados en la Cuenta Pública 2021.\n\n\nLos programas prioritarios en su conjunto han experimentado un crecimiento presupuestal conside-\nrable, el 70 % en términos reales. Sin embargo, se encontró que la asignación presupuestaria de los\nprogramas prioritarios no está determinada por el cumplimiento de sus objetivos. Prueba de ello es\nque los dos programas con mayores presupuestos, S176 de Bienestar y U013 de Salud, alcanzaron un\nCEM opuesto, siendo el primero destacado y el segundo deficiente.\n\n\nAdemás, se observó que todavía existen dificultades en la identificación de las temáticas de atención\ny población objetivo. Tres de los programas que no han definido de manera clara y concreta su temá-\ntica, tampoco logran identificar a su población objetivo, estos son los siguientes:\n\n\n     •    U083-Universidades para el Bienestar (SEP)\n     •    S281-Programa Nacional de Reconstrucción (Sedatu)\n     •    U013-Atención a la Salud y Medicamentos Gratuitos (Salud)\n\n\nFinalmente, otro reto importante de los programas prioritarios es seguir perfeccionando sus medicio-\nnes de cobertura, al mejorar el registro de sus beneficiarios y mantener la comparabilidad de esta\nmedición en el tiempo.\n\n\n\n\n                         50      Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2022: estado actual al cuarto año de la Administración y resultados alcanzados en 2021", "excerpt_sha256": "65e4850669fceec48f4dfc28ed524099f1d78f9277fabe38fde1eb352b406543", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0052-E001", "source_id": "SRC-9a96a4fbd3b1a6f5", "url": "https://www.coneval.org.mx/InformesPublicaciones/Documents/Informe_Programas_Prioritarios_2023.pdf", "title": "Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2023", "publisher": "CONEVAL", "raw_sha256": "fca21ecce6ebb306d276d4e77de72438ea1fc2a4682148a26f6a8377e710c60f", "fact_summary": "CONEVAL reports that 16 priority programs received 512,805 million pesos in 2023, a real increase of 20 percentage points over the 2019 priority-program group, which differed from the 2023 group; their share of social-program spending rose to more than 40%. La\n     razón para recurrir a este periodo radica en su continuidad: los programas de 2023 ya estaban activos\n     en 2020, a diferencia de 2019, como se explicó en el capítulo 1. Para 2023, este porcentaje aumentó a más de 40 %. Esto implica que, de cada\n     peso destinado a iniciativas sociales, más de 40 centavos se dirigen a los programas prioritarios de la\n     administración vigente.\n\n\n     Gráfica 1. that 13 of 16 priority programs adequately identified their target population in 2023, but three did not. Por lo tanto, en este capítulo se hace un análisis de las poblaciones objetivo aten-\n     didas por los 16 programas prioritarios vigentes en 2023, a partir de los factores relevantes contenidos\n     en sus objetivos de Propósito.\n\n\n     ¿Qué grupos poblacionales están atendiendo los programas prioritarios?\n\n     La clave para lograr un desarrollo social efectivo comienza con la identificación precisa del problema\n     público que los programas se proponen resolver. El siguiente paso crucial es determinar qué pobla-\n     ción objetivo se va a atender con base en los criterios de elegibilidad establecidos en la normativa del\n     programa y en qué medida se espera lograr el cambio o resultado. En relación con los 16 programas\n     prioritarios vigentes para 2023, la gráfica 3 revela que 81 % (13) identifica adecuadamente a su pobla-\n     ción objetivo, mientras que 19 % (3) aún no.\n\n\n     Gráfica 3. ¿Cuántos programas prioritarios identifican su población objetivo en 2023?\n\n\n\n\n                                             19% (3)\n\n\n                                                                                                     El programa identifica adecuadamente\n                                                                                                     a su población objetivo\n\n                                                                                                     El programa no identifica a su población objetivo\n\n                                                                              81% (13)\n\n\n\n\n     Fuente: elaboración del CONEVAL.\n\n\n\n     A partir de la población objetivo que aparece en el Propósito de cada programa social, en 2023 se\n     identificaron siete grupos poblacionales, mismos que se muestran en la figura 4. Estos grupos son: 1)\n     Adultos mayores de 65 años o más, 2) Estudiantes, 3) Productores, 4) Personas con discapacidad, 5) Jó-\n     venes, 6) Población en condición de vulnerabilidad y 7) Niñas, niños y adolescentes. La octava catego-\n     ría de clasificación comprende los programas que No identifican con claridad a su población objetivo.\n\n     8\n          Como se señaló, la fecha de corte de la información de la MIR es el 26 de abril de 2023.\n\n\n\n\n17       Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2023\n         Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social\n\nFigura 4.
